Prompt
# Creative Direction & Architecture: The Floating Island & Living Sky Portfolio I am building a personal portfolio with a unique atmospheric narrative. I have attached the current starting files from my repository (`Hero.tsx`, `CodedSky.tsx`, `TimeDial.tsx`, `AtmosphericHeader.tsx`, `timeOfDay.ts`, `useTimeOfDay.ts`). Right now, these files only have a basic 4-step discrete sky and flat layers. I want you to rebuild this into the refined artistic vision and interactive sky engine detailed below. --- ## 1. The Core Scene & The "Floating Island" Reveal ### The Hero (Top of Page) * **The Setting:** An open, peaceful prairie knoll with wild tall grasses and flowers. Sitting in the middle is a vintage work desk with a laptop and a designer working. * **The Horizon:** Behind the desk and grass is an infinite, expansive sky. * **The Art Style:** The character and field are designed in an editorial geometric illustration style with heavy risograph stipple grain, dither, and tactile texture (avoiding generic flat vector corporate art). ### The Spatial Twist on Scroll (The Floating Island) * As the user begins scrolling down, the perspective pulls down to reveal that this prairie field is not grounded on Earth—**it is a floating island suspended high in the stratosphere**. * The scroll uncovers the underside of the island: earthy rock strata, roots, weathered stone, and wisps of mist trailing into the open air. * Once the floating island scrolls up and out of view, **the infinite sky remains as the continuous background for the entire website**. * As the visitor scrolls into Selected Works, the case study cards float across this living sky like precise, architectural glass pavilions with crisp hairline borders and Swiss typography—creating a stunning contrast between the soft, organic clouds behind and the hyper-rigid design structure in front (inspired by the editorial discipline of Contra Labs). --- ## 2. The Living Sky & Timelapse Engine (Key Decisions) ### Continuous Timelapse Scrubbing (Not Static Steps) * The sky should not just jump between 4 rigid steps. It must operate on a **continuous 24-hour arc**. * By default, it auto-syncs with the visitor’s real local time. * It features an interactive **Time Dialer / Scrubber**: when the visitor grabs the dial and drags it back and forth, the sky moves like a **cinematic timelapse**: - The sun and moon follow a continuous parabolic arc across the horizon. - The stars softly twinkle and fade in as night falls. - The atmospheric gradients shift smoothly from dawn blue hour (`06:00`), to midday clarity (`12:00`), to fiery terracotta sunset (`18:00`), to dusk violet (`20:30`), and deep midnight indigo (`02:00`). - Colors must blend smoothly without dead grayish midtones (leveraging modern color spaces like OKLCH). ### Volumetric, Light-Reactive Clouds * The clouds must feel volumetric and tangible, not like static flat cutouts. * As you scrub the timelapse dial, **the clouds actively react to the sun's position and lighting**: - At sunrise: warm pink and amber under-lighting. - At midday: bright white, high-contrast illumination. - At sunset: fiery peach and golden rim lights. - At night: deep indigo silhouettes with soft silver moonlight rims. ### 3 Switchable Aesthetic Modes (To Test in Browser) I want the sky architecture to support **three switchable texture styles** via a simple prop or state flag (`variant="vector" | "dither" | "volumetric"`) so I can test and see what feels best: 1. **`vector`:** Pure, high-precision mathematical gradients and clean SVG/CSS drift. 2. **`dither`:** A stylized risograph/stipple dithered texture that directly mirrors the grain of the character illustration. 3. **`volumetric`:** Soft, layered cloud plates with deep atmospheric depth. --- ## 3. Your Task Using the provided prototype files as your foundation: 1. Re-architect `CodedSky.tsx` and the time system to support **continuous 24-hour timelapse scrubbing** with the celestial sun/moon arc, light-reactive clouds, and the three switchable texture modes. 2. Update the `TimeDial.tsx` HUD component so it acts as a fluid, tactile scrubber that smoothly drives the timelapse. 3. Structure `Hero.tsx` so the sky sits as the persistent canvas, and set up the layout structure for the **floating island diorama** (surface field → underside rock reveal → transition to floating project cards). build in the sky field folder and move all these files there as well.

The user is asking me to settle the architecture for a Vite + React 19 + TypeScript app at `/data/ws/Claude/Sky field`, keeping the six prototype component names so the user can compare directly, with coherent import paths, no heavy dependencies, and the scene written as procedural SVG instead of relying on missing raster imagery. I work the time engine first: `src/lib/timeOfDay.ts` holds a fractional-hour representation (`SkyTime`, 0–24), runs a smooth cycle across midnight, and stores OKLCH color keyframes with more stops near sunrise and sunset. The key interpolation trick is converting OKLCH to linear sRGB space and interpolating there, which avoids dead gray midtones and keeps out-of-gamut transitions saturated through the sunset ramp. The code splits into three concerns: a pure `celestial.ts` for sun/moon position math, a `lighting.ts` that derives shading values (elevation, azimuth, star opacity, cloud colors, haze, exposure, terrain tints) from sky time, and a `skyPalette.ts` for the OKLCH keyframe ramp and color utilities. That separation keeps the system testable and the UI components dumb. `CodedSky.tsx` is one component with a `variant` prop driving three approaches: a pure CSS gradient mesh with layered SVG clouds for `vector`, a WebGL shader for `dither` using ordered/blue-noise Bayer dithering to match the risograph aesthetic, and `volumetric` in between. Raymarching is expensive on weak hardware, so `volumetric` uses layered SVG/CSS plates with deep atmospheric depth and alpha-blended haze, applying a cloud light model that computes rim light and body tint from the sun direction. For `dither`, a WebGL2 fullscreen shader synthesizes a procedural sky gradient with 4x4 Bayer dithering and quantized riso ink layers, deriving clouds from simple fbm noise. A shared GLSL sky branch handles uniforms for palettes, sun/moon positions, time, variant, dims, DPR, with three colour-output branches. A `useWebGL` hook checks for context support and falls back to the vector DOM/SVG path. For clouds, the `volumetric` variant uses multiple soft ellipses with screen/soft-light blends tuned by the lighting model, avoiding tile artifacts that CSS blur causes in some browsers. `CodedSky.tsx` renders a container with stacked layers per variant; vector gets CSS gradient plus SVG cloud paths, a crisp sun/moon disc with glow; dither is canvas. Sun, moon, and stars are separate SVG/DOM elements positioned from the continuous celestial arc, so sky, hero, and header stay in sync as time changes. Dither's canvas handles stars and clouds itself, so the DOM celestial layer hides in that variant. Celestial math splits into `sunAltitude` (sine wave with peak altitude, night to day) and `sunAzimuth` (smooth daytime curve, continuing around the back at night). `moonPhase` is a synodic cycle from a known new moon, with `moonIllumination` and `moonAltitude`/`moonAzimuth` mirroring the sun formula phase-shifted. `moonPathPosition` feeds the arc; `starField` generates star positions with a seeded PRNG in normalized sky coordinates. The sky maps to a dome: horizontal x from azimuth, vertical y from altitude normalized to viewport. Not astronomically exact, but continuous, with a parallax arc matching the spec. Stars render on a seeded layer with opacity from sun elevation via smoothstep. `lighting.ts` derives values from `t` starting with `sunElevation`. `

Read all six files and set the folder up. No code beyond scaffolding yet — here's the plan and what I think you're actually after.

What I took from your idea

The through-line isn't "a portfolio with a sky background." It's one continuous place, lit by a real day, that the visitor scrolls through rather than past:

  • The hero starts intimate and grounded — desk, grass, a person working. Nothing signals "space."
  • The first scroll is a camera move, not a transition: the ground falls away and reveals you've been standing on a floating island the whole time. The reveal is the payoff, so it has to be one unbroken perspective pull with the horizon staying put, not a crossfade between two images.
  • After the island exits, the sky is no longer a backdrop — it's the page surface. Everything after lives in it. That's what makes the case-study cards work: rigid Swiss glass pavilions over soft organic cloud is a deliberate material collision, not decoration.
  • Time is a material, not a theme. The sky has to be lit like a physical place — one sun position that drives gradient, cloud lighting, terrain tint, star fade, and shadow, all from a single source of truth. Scrubbing the dial should feel like pulling film through a projector: continuous, inertial, no steps, no gray mush between sunset and night.
  • The risograph logic is a unifying print constraint — limited inks, halftone/dither, slight misregistration — applied to the character and the sky so both read as the same print run. That's what keeps it out of "flat corporate vector."

What I found in the code (constraints that shape the plan)

  • It's 4 discrete phases wired through a DOM CustomEvent (timeofdaychange) + a data-scenario attribute. Continuous scrubbing at 60fps can't ride on that event — it needs a real store (useSyncExternalStore with subscribable fractional-hour state), with the event kept as a compatibility shim.
  • The scene is two JPEG plates (hero-midground.jpg, hero-foreground.jpg) plus Tailwind classes (phasesky-cloud, hero-grade) that live in a globals.css I don't have, plus Grid, TransitionLink, gsap, and @/lib aliases from a Next.js app. So this is a port, not a drop-in.
  • That means I can't reuse your riso JPEGs — so the field, desk, figure, and island underside have to be authored as procedural SVG in that editorial stipple style. That's the biggest art risk, and I want your call on it before I burn time there.

Folder is staged as src/sky/, src/sections/, src/lib/, qa/, public/, with the original CodedSky.tsx kept at root as reference to diff against.

Planned architecture

FileJob
lib/timeOfDay.tsFractional hour t ∈ [0,24), wraps at midnight. Named anchors (06:00, 12:00, 18:00, 20:30, 02:00) as constants, plus legacy phase mapping for old consumers.
lib/skyPalette.tsOKLCH keyframe ramp with dense stops through sunrise/sunset, interpolated in linear-light / OKLab then gamut-mapped. This is what kills the dead gray midtones.
lib/celestial.tsPure math: sun elevation/azimuth along a parabolic arc, moon in antiphase, star opacity, moon phase. No DOM, unit-testable.
lib/lighting.tsDerives one LightModel from t — key light direction, ambient, cloud rim/body tints, terrain tint, exposure — consumed by sky, hero, island, and header so everything is lit by the same sun.
CodedSky.tsxTakes `variant="vector"
TimeDial.tsxPointer-capture drag scrubber with inertia, arrow-key stepping, grab-cursor, live HH:MM readout, "sync to local time" reset. Not four radio buttons.
useTimeOfDay.tsSubscribable store; server snapshot pinned to noon so hydration matches, real local time read after mount.
Hero.tsx300vh scroll stage: sticky canvas, island diorama (surface → rock strata/roots → mist), scroll-linked reveal with transform-only animation.
sections/Works.tsxIsland scrolls out, cards resolve in over the persistent sky.

Sequence: (1) time engine + palette + tests → (2) sky variants → (3) dial → (4) hero/island → (5) cards → (6) perf + reduced-motion + keyboard audit.

Three decisions I need from you

  1. Stack — the originals are Next.js App Router. I'd build Vite + React + TS (faster loop, no SSR/hydration headaches for local-time sync, easy static export). Say if you need Next specifically.
  2. Art — procedural SVG illustration in the riso stipple style (I can do this well, and it's the only option without your JPEGs), versus you supplying the plates later and me building the scene against placeholders. My preference is procedural, with clean layer seams so real art can replace any piece.
  3. Default variant — I'd ship volumetric as the default and keep dither one keystroke away, since volumetric gives the depth your spec leans on.

Thinking`Island.tsx` is procedural SVG under seeded RNG, split into `data-slot="surface"`, `figure`, `underside` and `mist` layers. The hero stage is sticky over 300vh, with progress 0–0.35 pulling down and scaling 1 → 0.92, 0.35–0.7 lifting the island out, and 0.7–1 handing off to the cards. Transforms ride on a `--p` variable written from a rAF scroll listener. The dial is a horizontal 24h scrubber with a sun/moon knob, arrow keys at ±15min, shift ±1h, Home/End, and momentum on release. Variants bind to keys 1/2/3 and persist in localStorage. The user is asking me to build the full site, so I work through the concrete decisions. I fix the stack: React 19, TypeScript, Vite, no GSAP. The file layout is a `package.json`, `tsconfig.json`, `vite.config.ts`, `index.html`, then `src/main.tsx`, `src/App.tsx`, `styles/global.css`, and a `lib/` folder holding `timeOfDay.ts`, `useTimeOfDay.ts`, `color.ts`, `skyPalette.ts`, `celestial.ts`, `lighting.ts`. The `sky/` folder holds `CodedSky.tsx`, `VectorSky.tsx`, `DitherSky.tsx`, `VolumetricSky.tsx`, `Clouds.tsx`, `Celestial.tsx`, `TimeDial.tsx`, `VariantSwitcher.tsx` and `grain.tsx`. The `sections/` folder holds `Hero.tsx`, `Island.tsx`, `Works.tsx` and `AtmosphericHeader.tsx`. I set the time store to a fractional hour in 0..24 with a module-level `current`, a `Set` of listeners, `setSkyTime(t, {source})`, and `useSyncExternalStore`, with server snapshot 12. Sun altitude is `alt = Math.sin(Math.PI * (t - 6) / 12)`. At t=0 this is sin(-π/2) = -1, at t=6 it is 0, at t=12 it is 1, at t=18 it is 0. Sun x is `50 - cos(π*(t-6)/12)*50`, giving 0 at t=6, 50 at t=12, 100 at t=18. Moon altitude is the negation, so moon x reads 0 at t=18, 50 at t=0, 100 at t=6. Moon phase comes from `(daysSinceNewMoon / 29.530588) % 1` and illumination `(1 - cos(2π*phase))/2`. For color I write OKLab to linear sRGB with the coefficients 0.3963377774, 0.2158037573, -0.1055613458, -0.0638541728, -0.0894841775, -1.2914855480, then 4.0767416621, -3.3077115913, 0.2309699292, -1.2684380046, 2.6097574011, -0.3413193965, -0.0041960863, -0.7034186147, 1.7076147010. Interpolation happens in OKLab, then I gamut-map by binary-searching chroma down until the result sits in sRGB, and output hex strings so CSS and canvas agree. Keyframe stops run 00:00, 02:00, 04:15, 05:15, 06:00, 06:45, 08:30, 12:00, 15:00, 17:15, 18:00, 18:45, 19:30, 20:30, 21:45, 23:00, each with zenith, mid, horizon, glow, haze and key colors. The light model carries `time`, `sunAlt`, `sunX`, `sunY`, `moonAlt`, `moonX`, `moonY`, `moonIllum`, `moonPhase`, `starOpacity`, `daylight`, `twilight`, `palette`, `cloud`, `tint`, `tintAmount`, `glowStrength`, `sunVisible` and `phase`. A single effect writes these as CSS variables on the root so only the structure re-renders; `DitherSky` subscribes to the store directly and draws on rAF. The dither canvas computes the gradient color per pixel, adds a Bayer 4×4 offset near 1/16, and quantizes each channel to 6 levels at roughly 480×270 scaled up with `image-rendering: pixelated`. The volumetric variant uses soft radial-gradient ellipse plates with a rim group offset toward the sun side, and the vector variant uses crisp bands and a small sun disc. `Island.tsx` is procedural SVG under seeded RNG, split into `data-slot="surface"`, `figure`, `underside` and `mist` layers. The user is asking me to work through the technical decisions for a sky-lighting and island-diorama interface. I start with fonts: no local fonts are available, so system-stack fonts plus @fontsource packages (Fraunces for display, Inter for body and UI, JetBrains Mono for labels) give offline reliability, and I install @fontsource-variable/fraunces, @fontsource-variable/inter and @fontsource-variable/jetbrains-mono. I then set up package.json, tsconfig, the Vite config, index.html and the lib files. In src/lib/timeOfDay.ts I define SkyTime as fractional hours 0..24, with anchors dawn 6, midday 12, sunset 18, dusk 20.5, midnight 2, and helpers clampTime, parseTime ("18:30" to 18.5), formatTime, localNow, phaseAt and isNight. The store in useTimeOfDay.ts defaults to 12, reads localNow() on mount, uses useSyncExternalStore with a server snapshot of 12, and dispatches a timeofdaychange event carrying {t, phase}. It keeps an autoSync flag that turns off after a dial scrub, with a 30-second tick, and exports both useSkyTime and useTimeOfDay. In src/lib/color.ts I convert through OKLCH to OKLab to linear sRGB to sRGB, with gamut mapping by binary search on chroma, plus mixOkLCH, sampleGradient, withAlpha, lerp, smoothstep and clamp. Mixing in OKLab keeps the hues rich so the midtones do not go gray. src/lib/skyPalette.ts holds the keyframes: midnight zenith l .16 c .06 h 275; pre-dawn 04:00 l .30; blue hour 05:00 horizon l .5 c .08 h 262; dawn 06:00 rose horizon l .8 c .07 h 40; sunrise 06:45 amber l .8 c .13 h 55; morning 08:30 zenith l .72 c .09 h 235; midday zenith l .7 c .11 h 245; golden 17:15 horizon l .82 c .11 h 70; terracotta sunset 18:00 horizon l .68 c .16 h 42 and zenith l .5 c .09 h 300; afterglow 18:45 l .58 c .15 h 25; dusk 19:30 l .42 c .1 h 310; night 21:45 zenith .14 c .05 h 270. Each stop also carries sunCore, sunGlow, moon, cloudLit, cloudShadow, cloudRim, terrain and haze. src/lib/celestial.ts places the sun with x = (1 - cos(π(t-6)/12))/2 * 100 and alt = sin(π(t-6)/12), mapping altitude to horizonY minus alt times the arc height. The moon is at antiphase, rising at 18:00 and setting at 06:00, and I render it as a shaded disc rather than an exact terminator. src/lib/lighting.ts exposes getLight(t) returning the full LightModel and cssVars(light) writing custom properties such as --sky-zenith, --sun-x, --moon-y and --cloud-rim. The dither canvas runs at 320x180: vertical sky gradient, sun glow, seeded stars, fbm clouds in 3 tonal bands, then Bayer 4x4 selection between the 2 nearest riso inks, with paper grain, at about 20fps and paused when offscreen or in the other variant. Volumetric clouds are SVG puff clusters with per-cloud drift and blur by depth. The island is one wrapper with surface and underside layers, scaling from 1 to 0.55 as the hero scrolls through 320vh.

The user is asking me to build the three sky variants. I check `arcX(t, riseAt)` with `wrapHours` and a 12h span: at t=6, `(6-6+24)%24 = 0` gives span 0, so x=0; at t=18, span is 1, x=1. For t=0, span is `(0-6+24)%24 / 12 = 18/12 = 1.5`, giving `0.5 - 0.5*cos(1.5π) = 0.5`, the zenith, which is correct. At t=3, span is `21/12 = 1.75`, cos(1.75π) ≈ 0.707, so x ≈ 0.146, still fine. The sun function calls `arcX(t, 6)` on both branches of a ternary that are identical, so that call is redundant and I simplify it. For the moon, `arcX(t, 18)` at t=0 gives 0.5 with altitude -1 at midnight, which checks out. For the variants: the vector sky uses absolutely positioned layers with a zenith-to-haze gradient, an SVG radial glow, a crisp disc, and a cloud group driven by a data array with drift animations and colors from `--sky-cloud-lit` and `--sky-cloud-rim`. I'm designing an island scene as procedural SVG art in an editorial riso stipple style, on a 1440×900 viewBox. The scene has four layered groups — surface, figure, underside, mist — each a named slot. The sky-facing surface begins at y≈610: a curved grassy knoll with a domed top and a rim that flares wider at the edge, drawn in sage-olive (#7d8c5f, adjusted by lighting). I seed roughly 220 short strokes along the top curve for grass tufts, each with its own tail and varying height, plus tiny ochre and cream flower heads. On the crown I center a vintage mid-century wooden desk with a two-toned top, tapered legs and a small drawer, holding an open laptop, a mug and a lamp. The figure is a seated designer in side profile, flat geometric shapes with minimal facial detail (a ponytail dot), in rust or indigo with visible stipple grain. The underside is a tapered rock body narrowing to a point, with strata bands in earth tones (topsoil, clay, sandstone, schist), jagged edges, dangling roots, moss at the lip and mist wisps. The generator is seeded and reusable: it produces all geometry as data, then renders SVG with fractal noise turbulence for stipple grain, a displacement map for wobble, and a halftone dot pattern masked over the terrain. Translucent tint rects over each group let lighting variables adjust the whole illustration. The component exports Scene, Surface, Figure and Underside pieces inside a single `<svg>` container, built as Island.tsx in the Sky field folder. Card width scales down to one column on mobile, keeping the floating parallax.

ThinkingThe user is asking for the final response after the reset, covering what changed, the current state,
The user is asking for the final response after the reset, covering what changed, the current state,

Reset done. Here's where it landed.

What changed

Removed the illustration layer entirely. Island.tsx no longer draws a figure, desk, lamp, laptop, grass or rock strata. Nothing in the build draws human anatomy or organic terrain in SVG anymore.

The hero is now a framing container. The island is built as three planes — a lit surface, a shadowed rock face, a base that dissolves into mist — with a dedicated .hero-art-slot positioned over it, moving on the same scroll parallax and inheriting the sky grade. Dropping in your editorial cutout is a one-node change; nothing else moves.

The sky was rebuilt for atmospheric depth:

  • Gradient stops now crowd toward the horizon, mirroring how air mass accumulates at low altitude — that's what makes it go pale at the base instead of fading evenly.
  • A wide elliptical horizon glow follows the sun's x position and falls off fast — scattering, not a spotlight.
  • Clouds are multi-puff feathered plates at low opacity with heavy blur. No outlines, no vector silhouettes, no chunky dots.
  • Plates carry a shadow body under the lit body, so they stay present as density after sundown instead of vanishing.

The HUD is now one instrument. A 196px glass card pinned bottom-right: a 260° radial time dial (00:00 → 24:00), the moment label, three texture dots, and a fold control. No full-width bar, no "SKY: 1 VOLUMETRIC" debug row.

Verified in the browser

Capture matrix is in qa/shots/ — dawn, midday, golden hour, sunset, dusk, night, all three variants, both reveal stages, works section day and night, plus mobile at 390×844. Sunset, night, and mid-reveal are the strongest.

Honest gaps

  • The plateau is still the weakest element. It reads as a floating landmass now, but the underside is soft enough to lose definition at small sizes. That slot is exactly where your art replaces it — I'd not invest more in the SVG.
  • The dither variant is unchanged in character from before the reset; I didn't rework it for the new atmosphere. It still uses the old cloud silhouettes from Clouds.tsx, so it'll look inconsistent with the new volumetric sky until it's re-pointed at the new plate system.
  • Clouds.tsx is now used only by VectorSky and DitherSky — worth deciding whether to keep two cloud systems or unify.

Dev server is on http://localhost:5174. ?t=18:40&variant=dither reproduces any state.

Prompt
can we change the dialer that changes the sky with a clock like system, an analog and digital clock both , it shows the real time but changing or playing with it changed the background sky. i think it will be a new unique interaction for this type and different from all the reference, we need to do something new
Codex
ThinkingThe user is asking for a real analog clock face to replace the radial dial: draggable hour and minute hands, a digital readout, and a sky that responds as the hands move. I map the 12-hour face onto the 24-hour sky by keeping the current half-day and letting a full hand rotation advance 12 hours, tracking angular direction so wrapping past 12 adds or subtracts 12 hours. For the minute hand, angle/6 gives minutes 0-60, so t = floor(t) + minutes/60 handles the wrap on its own. Dragging the face computes one cumulative unwrapped delta angle: t = startTime + (deltaAngle/360)*12 hours, wrapped to 24; clockwise advances, counter-clockwise rewinds, one turn = 12 hours, which reads as winding the clock forward. A Shift modifier gives fine control at 1 hour per turn. The hour angle is ((t % 12)/12)*360 and the minute angle is ((t*60 % 60)/60)*360. A second hand runs on its own local interval reading localNow(), so only the Clock re-renders each second and the store time stays at 30s granularity, keeping the heavier sky and dither redraws from firing every tick. An outer 24h ring uses a conic-gradient masked to a thin band with a dot at the current time-of-day position for coarse scrubbing. The digital readout stays below with the live/sync toggle; keyboard arrows give ±15 min, shift ±1h, Home/End. This is a safe, ordinary UI request, so I help freely.

"use client";

/**
 * TimeArc — a radial instrument, not a slider.

 * A 260° arc maps the full day (00:00 → 24:00) around a single dial. Drag the
 * handle, use arrow keys, or press Now to re-sync with the visitor's clock.
 * The whole control is one small glass card pinned to the corner of the
 * viewport: location line, time, condition — the way a high-end HUD reads.
 *
 * Geometry: 12 o'clock is noon. The arc opens at the bottom (160° sweep gap),
 * so the handle never collides with the card's own labels.
 */

import { useCallback, useEffect, useRef, useState } from "react";

ThinkingThe user is asking for an analog clock interface with a digital readout that syncs to real time and controls the sky. I build `TimeClock.tsx` with an SVG dial, tick marks every 5 minutes, an hour hand, a minute hand, a seconds hand, and a 24-hour ring whose dot tracks the current position. The seconds hand reads local time without triggering store updates. I map angle to time: each full rotation equals 12 hours, clockwise drags advance time and counter-clockwise drags reverse it, so the full 24-hour cycle is reachable. The hour hand advances 12 hours per rotation, the minute hand 1 hour, both sharing one `timeBase` set on pointerdown. The control keeps slider ARIA attributes for keyboard and screen reader support. A digital row shows the time in monospace, the moment label, variant dots, and a reset button.
The user is asking for an analog clock interface with a digital readout that syncs to real time and controls the sky. I build `TimeClock.tsx` with an SVG dial, tick marks every 5 minutes, an hour hand, a minute hand, a seconds hand, and a 24-hour ring whose dot tracks the current position. The seconds hand reads local time without triggering store updates. I map angle to time: each full rotation equals 12 hours, clockwise drags advance time and counter-clockwise drags reverse it, so the full 24-hour cycle is reachable. The hour hand advances 12 hours per rotation, the minute hand 1 hour, both sharing one `timeBase` set on pointerdown. The control keeps slider ARIA attributes for keyboard and screen reader support. A digital row shows the time in monospace, the moment label, variant dots, and a reset button.

The clock metaphor needs one honest constraint: a face is 12 hours, a sky is 24. I'll resolve it this way — drag the hour hand and you sweep 12 hours per turn (two turns = a full day), drag the minute hand and you move ±1 hour per turn. Clockwise winds the day forward, counter-clockwise rewinds. The seconds hand is live and only shown when you're in sync with real time.

1. Island — Island.tsx is rebuilt as a wide, asymmetric prairie ridge with two offset swells, running past both viewport edges. The underside is now curved, organic crags (no sawtooth, no cone), with seven strata bands that dip and rise at increasing amplitude with depth, three erosion lines on the flanks, twenty-six roots hanging off the turf line, and a gradient mask that dissolves the base into mist. The hero crest sits in the bottom third.

2. Clouds — blur dropped from 12 + depth*38 (up to 50px) to 4 + depth*8 (4–12px). Each plate now has three layers: an offset ambient shadow base, the body, and a sun-facing rim of bright crescents. Horizon bloom cut roughly in half.

3. Cards — rgba(16, 20, 36, 0.85) obsidian slab, 1px rgba(255,255,255,0.12) hairline, inset 0 1px 0 rim light, 6px radius. Title is #FBF9F5, body at 80% alpha. Text shadows are gone — legibility now comes from the surface.

4. Palette — midday zenith moved from l 0.66 / c 0.115 / h 242 to l 0.56 / c 0.072 / h 250: slate air blue instead of electric cyan. Morning and afternoon retuned on the same curve, horizon haze kept warm.

5. Clock drag — the bug was onPointerLeave cancelling the drag as soon as the pointer left the thin hand shape. Capture now happens on the <svg> root, and clicking anywhere on the face grabs the hour hand. Verified: a 180° sweep moves 12:00 → 18:00.

One thing still off

At 45–60% scroll the island shrinks into a small ridge rather than revealing a floating mass. The pull-back scale (1 - p*0.46) is fighting the crest being full-bleed at rest, so the transition reads as "the hill recedes" rather than "the ground falls away." A proper fix means animating the wrapper's width from full-bleed down to ~55vw rather than scaling, so the flanks genuinely enter frame as a discrete object. I'd rather flag that than claim the reveal is finished.

For raymarched volumetric clouds, I'm weighing a WebGL approach using Beer-Lambert absorption, powder effect, and Henyey-Greenstein scattering for realistic lighting, rendered at half-resolution with a precomputed 3D Perlin-Worley noise texture generated either on CPU at startup or directly on GPU for speed. Since raymarching horizontally through the layer at that altitude could get expensive, I'm thinking of capping ray distance and growing step size with distance, adding fog for far clouds, but this still feels like a heavy shader build — so I'm reconsidering a cheaper approach instead. Rather than attempting sprite-based clouds, which would need real assets I don't have, I'm leaning toward a well-tuned volumetric raymarcher like iq's classic "Clouds" shader — using 3D noise textures with cheap directional-derivative lighting instead of full light marching, which stays performant at around 40-80 steps while still looking photoreal when paired with a physically-based sky. I want the sky and cloud coloring tied directly to the design's OKLCH palette rather than generic scattering colors, so I'm planning uniforms for zenith/horizon/haze/sun colors pulled from CSS variables, with cloud lighting computed as sun color times transmittance plus ambient bounce light, and a forward-scattering silver lining near the sun. Now I'm starting to sketch out the GLSL uniform declarations for resolution, time, sun/moon direction and color. I'm also toying with an idea where scrolling the page subtly shifts the camera pitch, so the viewer feels like they're rising up through clouds toward a floating island as they scroll—keeping it optional but intriguing. For the sky background itself, I'm computing color as a vertical gradient matching the CSS mapping (zenith to mid to horizon to haze) with an added sun glow term. I'm deciding to move everything—sky, sun glow, moon phase shading, and even a twinkling star field—entirely into the shader rather than relying on separate DOM elements, since celestial objects need to render behind the clouds and a single canvas keeps compositing simple. The tricky part is making sure the sun's position in the shader matches the CSS custom properties already driving other layers like the horizon glow. I'm realizing that if the sun dips below the horizon in the light model, the lighting direction still illuminates clouds from below, which actually produces a nice sunset effect with pink-lit cloud undersides. I need to check the lighting module to see what fields the sun position function actually returns, and I'm also weighing an alternative where the canvas only renders clouds with alpha transparency, letting the sky gradient and celestial DOM layer sit underneath — but then I need to figure out where the clouds should be layered relative to that. Stacking the sky gradient, celestial layer, and cloud canvas in that order should work, with fog toward the horizon handled by blending cloud color into the sky color while preserving alpha through premultiplication. Doing everything in-shader would give a more unified, photorealistic result with scattering and crepuscular rays, but keeping the existing DOM-based celestial layer and using a hybrid alpha approach preserves the current star/moon infrastructure while still letting cloud rendering look good. But the flat CSS gradient can't dither to avoid banding or produce proper Mie halos and horizon brightening -- and having stars rendered in DOM above an opaque cloud canvas means they'd wrongly show through clouds at night. So I'm deciding to move the entire sky into shader: sky, sun, moon with phase, stars, clouds, horizon -- all computed together so clouds can properly occlude celestial bodies. Now I'm thinking about performance: rendering at half resolution with adaptive scaling based on measured frame time. I'm leaning toward a two-pass approach — render clouds at low resolution to a texture, then composite them with the full-resolution sky, stars, and sun disc in a second pass, so stars and the sun stay sharp while clouds can be upsampled without looking bad. Since clouds drift slowly, I don't need to worry much about pausing for performance, and rAF naturally handles hidden tabs; the sky background stays fixed so visibility isn't an issue. I'm also considering using blue-noise or hashed jitter on the ray start offset to reduce banding artifacts over time. Generating CPU noise on the order of 262k voxels would cost roughly 100-200ms, which is acceptable if done once at init. But a cheaper trick is iq's classic 2D-texture-encodes-3D-noise approach: build a 256² texture where the green channel is the red channel offset in space, letting a single texture lookup approximate 3D value noise cheaply. I'm working out the indexing so that rg.x and rg.y correctly correspond to the noise value at lattice level z and z+1, tracing through the swizzle and offset relationship to define value(x,y,z) consistently. With this texture set to 256x256, REPEAT wrap, and LINEAR filtering (no mipmaps), bilinear interpolation naturally handles the xy smoothing, so filling it with random bytes is cheap and sufficient. For the actual cloud look, I'm considering that plain fbm value noise (like iq's approach with 5 octaves) gives soft, smoky clouds, but I want a more "cauliflower" cumulus billow shape, which probably needs something more Worley-like mixed in. I'm thinking of modeling cumulus density as a low-frequency coverage map in xz carving out where clouds exist, then shaping each cloud with a vertical density ramp — flat-ish base rising to a rounded top, with top height scaling with local coverage strength — and eroding the billows with higher-frequency turbulence noise to get that puffy, rounded lobe look rather than smoky wisps. For lighting, I need to march toward the sun with a handful of exponentially spaced samples for self-shadowing via Beer's law, which gives clouds their defined light/dark sides. But doing the math, full octaves at every step would mean hundreds of millions of texture fetches per frame—way too expensive for integrated GPUs at 60fps—so I should cut octaves for the light march and only use full detail in the primary march when density is significant. I'm also weighing adaptive stepping (large steps in empty space, fine steps inside clouds) against lower resolution rendering, since clouds get blurry when upsampled and the reference look is crisp and detailed. Another option is decoupling frame rates—rendering clouds at 20-30fps since they move slowly and only recompositing when the sun direction changes—or falling back to a cheaper 2D layered fbm approach with faked lighting for a photoreal look without full volumetric cost. Comparing against the classic shadertoy 2D cloud techniques, those look decent but too cartoonish compared to my target reference, so I'm committing to volumetric rendering with an adaptive quality scaler to manage performance. I want the scene to feel alive, with animated noise offsets for wind drift and a moving sun that shifts lighting through the day. For the camera setup, I'm thinking about placing it just below the cloud base altitude so I can see flat, darker cloud undersides overhead while cumulus shapes rise toward the horizon. Rather than the ground-level "infinite sky" look, I want something more dreamlike — camera positioned mid-level within a cumulus layer, with a clear zone nearby so cloud towers appear at moderate distance on both sides, fading into haze at the horizon, with a cloud sea visible below. I'm working out the layer boundaries, setting the base around -0.6 km and the top near 2.4 km relative to the camera. Tracing how rays behave at different elevations: near the horizon they travel long distances through the dense cloud slab producing thick fog-like coverage, while upward rays exit the top of the slab after a few kilometers, and near the top of the screen they stay inside the slab long enough to reveal gaps in coverage as pockets of visible sky. I'm working out the vertical field of view and horizon placement using the screen's pitch angle, solving for where the horizon falls in normalized coordinates and roughly getting an 18° upward pitch with the screen top around 43° elevation, so I can keep the sun's position consistent with the screen-space coordinates used in the CSS. Given the sun's x is clamped between 8–92% of screen width, it stays within the camera's horizontal field of view, meaning clouds near the sun get backlit with a strong silver lining effect rather than lit from the side. At noon the sun sits higher on screen, which would make backlit clouds look dramatic but darker in the center—though the reference image shows clouds lit more from the front with pastel tones, so I need to reconcile that difference. Since the sun disc is visible on screen, the light has to genuinely originate from there, so backlit clouds with glowing rims and darker interiors is actually physically correct and matches golden-hour photography well. To keep the cloud bodies from looking too dark, I'll boost the ambient scattering term, using a multi-octave approximation that sums several attenuated scattering passes with progressively reduced extinction coefficients to simulate multiple scattering. For day-night transitions, I'm handling moonlit night scenes with dim bluish light, and twilight scenes where the sun sits just below the horizon and lights cloud undersides pink while lower cloud parts fall into Earth's shadow—approximating this with a smoothstep based on sun elevation and sample altitude. I also want to fold in the art-directed color palette to guide these lighting choices. Mapping out the shader approach: the sky gradient uses the zenith/mid/horizon/haze colors blended by elevation in linear RGB space, with added Mie scattering glow around the sun and a horizon glow for realism. Sun lighting color derives from sunCore/sunGlow or a warming model at low altitude, while clouds use cloudLit for sun-facing surfaces, cloudShadow for ambient shadow, and cloudRim for the silver-lining effect. For cloud color, I'm combining sunlit intensity (cloudLit scaled by sun energy, transmittance, and phase function) with ambient light blended between cloudShadow at the base and zenith color at the top, plus a rim contribution from forward scattering. Thinking through cloud geometry: cumulus puffs should span roughly 1-2km horizontally and 2-3km in height for towering shapes, with coverage noise cells around 2.5km wide. For the cauliflower look I'm weighing Perlin-Worley noise — inverting Worley cellular noise gives rounded blobs, and layering fbm on top produces that bumpy cauliflower detail. Doing full 3D Worley per-pixel in the shader is too costly with 27 neighbor cell checks, so I'm thinking about precomputing a tileable 3D noise texture instead, either on the CPU at startup (accepting some load-time jank) or by rendering it slice-by-slice on the GPU. I'm leaning toward the GPU slice approach using WebGL2's layered framebuffer rendering into a 64³ RGBA8 3D texture, packing a low-frequency shape channel plus several higher-frequency Worley fbm channels for detail, then combining and remapping those channels when sampling density in the cloud shader. Now I'm adding a second, higher-frequency detail texture for erosion (following the Horizon Zero Dawn/Nubis style pipeline), along with a vertical density gradient that rounds off cloud tops and bottoms, and a 2D weather map driving coverage so towering clouds form where the weather signal is strong. Working out the lighting model now: marching a few steps toward the light with cheap shape-only density to get optical depth, applying Beer's law attenuation, a multi-octave scattering approximation with phase function, a subtle powder effect for darker edges facing the sun, and ambient occlusion blending between bottom and top sky colors. For color sourcing, I'm pulling sun light from the palette's lit-cloud color and ambient from the shadow/zenith colors, then combining them with transmittance using an energy-conserving integration scheme along the view ray. For distant clouds, I'm blending their color toward the sky color at the view direction based on distance, so far-off clouds fade into horizon haze — computed via a shared sky function used in both the cloud pass and the final composite. The composite layer draws stars, sun, moon, and sky, while the cloud pass outputs premultiplied, fog-blended radiance with alpha as one minus transmittance, so clouds naturally occlude the sun disc when composited. I'm also adding a post-composite glow around the sun that bleeds faintly over cloud edges, scaled partly by transmittance, to mimic lens glare for a more photographic look. I'm adding dithering noise to prevent color banding and a subtle vignette, then working in linear space so I can tone-map the HDR sun brightness with a soft highlight rolloff curve that keeps midtones nearly identity while compressing values above a threshold for a natural photographic falloff. Now I'm moving on to writing a tileable 3D noise shader combining Perlin and Worley noise, starting with a hash function for lattice points and a Worley distance function that searches neighboring cells... Writing Worley noise function... Still writing the noise functions... In that shadertoy's cloud density function, it samples the precomputed noise texture and combines the worley fbm channels into a remap of the perlin-worley value, then remaps again against a coverage threshold to shape the cloud edges. For my implementation I'm planning a 64³ texture for the base shape (perlin freq 4, worley freq 4/8/16) and a smaller 32³ detail texture with higher-frequency worley fbm in the G/B/A channels, possibly reusing the shape texture instead. Generation cost on the GPU for this noise sampling is trivial. I'm also planning a fallback to CSS volumetric rendering if 3D texture setup fails, like a framebuffer incomplete error. I'm computing the exit distance based on the ray direction's vertical component, clamping to a max distance, and using an adaptive step size that grows further from camera. When density samples come back zero, I'm stepping forward more aggressively to save cost before refining near actual cloud material. I'm capping primary steps around 72 with a lighter 5-step light march, exiting early once transmittance drops below a threshold. At long range, fog takes over from cloud detail, so I'm making sure the horizon reads as a hazy cloud sea rather than sharp geometry, with near-horizon rays passing through a full slab thickness and lower-angle rays exiting through the base much closer in. Gaps below the base need to show a mirrored haze color rather than nothing, matching the sky function for negative elevation. For motion, I'm tying noise sampling to a slow wind drift (roughly 10-20 m/s equivalent) plus a separate time-varying offset for detail noise so the clouds billow and evolve rather than just translating, keeping the scene visibly alive without being distracting. I like the idea of linking cloud drift to the scrubbable sky-time itself, so dragging the clock forward makes clouds race by like a timelapse, with a scale factor like ~2.5km of drift per sky-hour. The tricky part is the 24→0 wraparound causing a jarring 60km jump in cloud position, so I need to accumulate time in an unwrapped way to avoid that pop. Temporal reprojection to allow quarter-res tracing is tempting but too complex for now, so I'll skip it. For performance monitoring, I'll avoid calling gl.finish since it stalls, and instead track rAF frame deltas: if the average exceeds 22ms over 30 frames I'll drop resolution scale by 15% down to a minimum of 0.3, and raise it back up if frame times stay under 17ms for 120 frames. ThinkingSince the ray-distance fade now handles pocket effects, coverage is independent of it, and TAA lets me cut step counts further. I'm also considering dropping idle framerate to 24fps given how slowly clouds drift, though 30 should be fine with TAA blending. Time to implement these changes.

I need to fix that boundary falloff so it doesn't read as a drawn outline. Instead of a cylinder cut, I could push the march start distance out to around 1.6km, effectively ignoring everything within that radius — but that's really a sphere cut, which would slice clouds into flat faces. Better to apply a smooth radial fade on density based on ray distance (sstep between roughly 0.8 and 2.6km) since rays are naturally radial from the camera, avoiding the tangent-ray artifacts entirely while still letting shadowing work normally. Checking the mobile sunrise shot, the sun disc looks dull gray rather than bright — likely because the radial scrim gradient in the corner is darkening that region rather than any cloud opacity issue, since the computed sun altitude and opacity at that time should be near full. Moving to the night scene, the stars and moon with halo look good, and the moon's gibbous shadow at 72% illumination reads correctly with the flattened left edge. The night clouds have a smoky, fibrous texture from unconverged noise passes, which looks acceptable, and the overall star density seems realistic rather than excessive. For priority fixes going forward: replacing the cylindrical pocket shape with a ray-distance fade, adding shape anisotropy for stacked cloud lobes, and introducing golden-ratio temporal jitter. The noon view with clouds behind cards looks fine as-is, though I don't have a way to verify actual GPU performance without real hardware testing. Thinking through the step budget, I'm realizing horizon-grazing rays with the current step-growth formula likely hit the 110-step cap around 14-15km distance rather than reaching the full atmosphere exit, meaning clouds farther out along those rays simply won't get sampled. But that's mostly fine since fog density is already quite heavy by 15km anyway, so those distant regions would be obscured regardless — the ray just falls back to sky color there, which looks acceptable. For in-cloud steps without skipping, the cost per sample is higher: the clear-sky steps need a few cheap texture fetches for density, while in-cloud steps add a detail noise fetch plus a light-marching loop for shadowing. Estimating the total fetches per pixel across a typical frame at the reduced render resolution, I land somewhere in the 10-20ms range on an integrated GPU like the M1, which is borderline but workable — especially since adaptive resolution scaling can cut that further by rendering at an even smaller fraction when needed. On weaker integrated GPUs like Intel UHD, performance could drop to around 20ms even with minimal scale, which is acceptable but might stress fans on a portfolio hero. I'm now considering optimizations: dropping the second weather fetch to save roughly 20% of the cost, reducing the light march step count on desktop, and adding empty-space skipping so pixels with zero cloud coverage can bail out early. I need to rethink the measurement to track actual frame cost rather than raw interval between render calls, perhaps by checking if the rAF callback following a render comes back late. So the plan is to track whether a render happened, measure the delta to the next rAF only when the previous frame rendered, and feed that into the renderer's sampling method, comparing against thresholds that account for display refresh rate so 120Hz screens aren't incorrectly flagged as overloaded. I'm also noting that GPU timer query extensions are unreliable across browsers, so sticking with rAF interval timing is the simpler path. For reduced-motion mode, since there's no continuous rendering to let TAA history converge, I need a settle mechanism: when content changes, set a counter so it keeps re-rendering for roughly 8-10 frames to let the temporal blend stabilize before going idle again. ThinkingAlso considering bumping TAA frame counts for QA screenshots since real-time convergence is much faster at 30-60fps, and noting the blocky dithering in the right cloud is just expected unconverged noise at 8 frames. Time to actually implement the jittered sampling.
The banding looks like terraced contour rings from raymarch slicing — likely the step size is too large relative to feature detail, and the jitter pattern isn't converging well with only 8 passes to properly break up the artifacts. I realize I removed the back-up logic when entering the cloud after skipping ahead, so the march lands at quantized step positions that create visible terraces. Since the step size grows with distance but the jitter amplitude stays roughly fixed, jitter becomes negligible compared to the step at larger distances, which explains why the concentric shells show up — the fix is to scale jitter proportionally with the step size at each point along the ray. Since variable strides break any clean closed-form jitter phase across the geometric recurrence, I'm leaning toward a simpler fix: sample each position at t_n plus the local step size times a per-pixel jitter factor, so the offset naturally scales with distance without needing to track accumulated phase. Refining the stride logic... Also considering bumping TAA frame counts for QA screenshots since real-time convergence is much faster at 30-60fps, and noting the blocky dithering in the right cloud is just expected unconverged noise at 8 frames. Time to actually implement the jittered sampling.

I've identified the issue: raymarch slicing from a fixed jitter that's too small relative to the large steps at 8km altitude, causing banding artifacts. I'll fix this by jittering each sample by a fraction of its own step size and backing up one fine step when entering cloud after a long stride.

cd "/data/ws/mimo/Sky field" && cat README.md
# Sky Field

A portfolio suspended in a living sky. Continuous 24-hour light, a floating
island diorama, and project cards that sit in the air like glass pavilions.

Vite + React 19 + TypeScript. No UI framework, no animation library — plain
React, plain CSS custom properties, procedural SVG.

```bash
npm install
npm run dev        # http://localhost:5173
npm run build
```

## The idea in one line

One continuous place, lit by one sun, that you scroll *through*: grounded
prairie → the ground falls away and reveals a floating island → the island
leaves and the sky becomes the page.

The sky is now rendered photographically in real time, the clock is a one-turn 24-hour dial, and the digital readout can be scrolled, dragged or typed into. I've sent you sky-day-cycle.png (five times of day plus the clock in each state) and sky-field-export.zip (everything needed to run it).

The sky

Contra's sky is pre-recorded cloud video, which only ever shows one moment. Yours is drawn live by a WebGL engine, so the same realism follows the clock through all 24 hours.

  • Real 3D clouds: the camera sits inside a layer of cumulus around the floating island. Clouds have flat bases, stacked towers, wispy edges, and fade into haze with distance.
  • Real lighting: the clouds are lit by the actual sun position. You get bright edges when looking toward the sun and pink undersides after sunset. At night they're moonlit, the moon shows its real phase, and there are stars and a faint Milky Way.
  • Same palette as before: it uses your existing colour track, so the island, cards and HUD stay lit consistently.
  • It moves: clouds drift slowly, race past like a timelapse when you scrub the clock, and you rise through them as you scroll.
  • Readability: a soft dark gradient now sits behind the hero text, because bright clouds can pass behind it.
  • Fallback: browsers without WebGL2 automatically get the old CSS clouds.
  • Palette fix: dawn and late afternoon were briefly passing through teal; that's fixed.

The clock

  • One turn = one day. Midnight is at the bottom, sunrise left, noon top, sunset right, and a sun or moon rides the ring.
  • Press anywhere on the ring and the sky glides there; keep dragging and it follows. No winding.
  • Digital readout in the centre: scroll or drag the hours or minutes up and down, or click and type a time ("7", "730", "19:45", "7pm", "noon").
  • Two buttons: ▶ plays the day as a timelapse, and "Back to now" returns to live time.
  • Every change eases smoothly rather than jumping.

I tested this with real mouse and keyboard input, and all interactions worked:

  • dragging from 18:00 through midnight to 02:00;
  • the wheel adding 3 hours;
  • dragging the minutes up 15;
  • typing "7:30pm", which landed on 19:30;
  • the timelapse playing, and back-to-now returning to live time.

The production build passes, and a fresh install from the zip builds cleanly.

Things to know

  • Speed on a real computer is untested. My test browser draws graphics without a GPU (about 1 frame per second), so I could only judge how it looks. The sky lowers its own resolution if frames run slow and drops to 30 fps when idle, but please open it on your laptop and tell me if it stutters or the fan spins up.
  • The island now looks flat next to a photographic sky. It's still the vector placeholder for your hero art, and it's the obvious next thing to bring up to the same standard.

Repo structure

Sky field/
├── index.html, package.json, package-lock.json, tsconfig.json, vite.config.ts
├── README.md                  how it works, porting to Next.js, QA
├── src/
│   ├── main.tsx, App.tsx      entry; fixed sky + page + HUD
│   ├── lib/                   time store (with easing), palette, colour maths,
│   │                          sun/moon positions, light model
│   ├── sky/
│   │   ├── webgl/             NEW: SkyRenderer.ts + shaders.ts (the photographic sky)
│   │   ├── PhotoSky.tsx       NEW: React wrapper for the WebGL sky
│   │   ├── TimeClock.tsx      REWRITTEN: 24h dial + digital readout
│   │   ├── CodedSky.tsx       chooses which sky style to show
│   │   ├── VolumetricSky, DitherSky, VectorSky, Celestial, Clouds
│   │   └── TimeDial.tsx, VariantSwitcher.tsx   old controls, nothing uses them
│   ├── sections/              Hero, Island, Works, AtmosphericHeader
│   └── styles/global.css      all styling, plain CSS
└── qa/                        headless-Chrome test scripts, renders/, shots/

What to copy to run it locally

NeededNot needed (generated)
package.json, package-lock.json, index.html, tsconfig.json, vite.config.ts, src/**, README.mdnode_modules/ (74 MB), dist/, tsconfig.tsbuildinfo, qa/shots/ and qa/renders/ (screenshots)

The qa/*.mjs test scripts are optional and need Google Chrome installed.

To run it: unzip sky-field-export.zip, then npm install, then npm run dev, and open http://localhost:5173. You need Node 20.19 or newer.

For Next.js, copy src/lib, src/sky, src/sections and global.css; everything already works there. The README walks through it step by step.

That fresh-install check caught one real bug, now fixed: the project depended on a package that package.json didn't list, so a clean install would have failed to build.
